[2402.09098] Multiple-output composite quantile regression through an optimal transport lens Multiple-output composite quantile regression through an optimal transport lens Xuzhi Yang Tengyao Wang Affiliation: Department of Statistics, London School of Economics February 14, 2024 Abstract Composite quantile regression has been used to obtain robust estimators of regression coefficients in linear models with good statistical efficiency. By revealing an intrinsic link between the composite quantile regression loss function and the Wasserstein distance from the residuals to the set of quantiles, we establish a generalization of the composite quantile regression to the multiple-output settings. Theoretical convergence rates of the proposed estimator are derived both under the setting where the additive error possesses only a finite ℓ \ell -th moment (for ℓ &gt; 2 \ell&gt;2 ) and where it exhibits a sub-Weibull tail. In doing so, we develop novel techniques for analyzing the M-estimation problem that involves Wasserstein-distance in the loss. Numerical studies confirm the practical effectiveness of our proposed procedure. Keywords: quantile regression, optimal transport, multivariate quantiles, robust estimation 1 Introduction The area of robust statistics has seen a revival of interest in recent years, both in Statistics and Computer Science. This is partly due to the fact that the massive surge in data volumes brings about a significant demand for efficient and precise analysis of heavy-tailed or partially corrupted data ( Eklund et al. 2016a ; Wang et al. 2015a ; Szegedy et al. 2014a ) . Compared to earlier works in this area pioneered by Tukey &amp; McLaughlin 1963a and Huber 1964a ; Huber 1965a , modern treatment of this topic focuses more on handling multivariate data. For instance, in the area of robust mean estimation, Diakonikolas et al. 2020a ; Lugosi &amp; Mendelson 2021a ; Depersin &amp; Lecué 2022a ; Minasyan &amp; Zhivotovskiy 2023a have proposed various extensions of univariate robust mean procedures such as the trimmed mean estimator ( Tukey &amp; McLaughlin 1963a ) and median of means estimator ( Nemirovskij &amp; Yudin 1983a ; Jerrum et al. 1986a ; Alon et al. 1996a ) to the multivariate setting. We witness a similar surge in research interest in the area of robust covariance estimation ( Mendelson &amp; Zhivotovskiy 2020a ; Abdalla &amp; Zhivotovskiy 2022a ; Minasyan &amp; Zhivotovskiy 2023a ) . In this work, we focus on the topic of robust linear regression with potentially multivariate response variable, where a covariate-response pair ( X , Y ) ∈ ℝ p × ℝ d (X,Y)\in\mathbb{R}^{p}\times\mathbb{R}^{d} with joint distribution P ( X , Y ) P^{(X,Y)} is generated from Y = b ∗ ​ X + ε , \displaystyle Y=b^{*}X+\varepsilon, (1) with regression coefficients b ∗ ∈ ℝ d × p b^{*}\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times p} , a zero-mean covariate vector X ∈ ℝ p X\in\mathbb{R}^{p} and a noise vector ε \varepsilon taking values in ℝ d \mathbb{R}^{d} . Given independent and identically distributed (i.i.d.​) covariate-response pairs ( X 1 , Y 1 ) , … ​ ( X n , Y n ) (X_{1},Y_{1}),\ldots(X_{n},Y_{n}) drawn from P ( X , Y ) P^{(X,Y)} , our goal is to estimate b ∗ b^{*} . The contamination of the a linear model is mainly captured by two different mechanisms: heavy-tailed noise ( Catoni 2012a ; Lugosi &amp; Mendelson 2019a ) and outlier contamination ( Szegedy et al. 2014a ; Huber 2004a ) . When d = 1 d=1 , both directions have thrived in recent years ( Nguyen &amp; Tran 2013a ; Fan et al. 2017a ; Sun et al. 2020a ; Sasai &amp; Fujisawa 2020a ; Pensia et al. 2020a ; Adomaityte et al. 2023a ) . However, in the context of multiple-output linear regression, where d &gt; 1 d&gt;1 , the literature is notably scant. In this work, we go beyond the case of the univariate response variable to the case of the multiple-output linear model under possibly heavy-tailed noise. One popular way to tackle the heavy-tailed error is based on the quantile regression ( Koenker &amp; Bassett 1978a ; Wang et al. 2007a ; Li &amp; Zhu 2008a ; Zou &amp; Yuan 2008a ; Wu &amp; Liu 2009a ; Belloni &amp; Chernozhukov 2011a ) . In the case of univariate linear regression, although the ordinary least square (OLS) estimator is widely recognized as the best unbiased estimator when the random error follows a Gaussian distribution since it attains the Cramer–Rao lower bound, it may not perform well when the random error is heavy-tailed, as the mean squared error of the OLS estimator is proportional to the second moment of the random error term. This issue can be addressed by using the quantile regression estimator ( Koenker &amp; Bassett 1978a ) . Unlike the OLS estimator, which estimates the conditional mean function, the quantile regression estimator aims to estimate the conditional quantile function of Y Y given X X . Thanks to the robustness of quantiles, the quantile regression estimator is less affected by outliers or heavy-tailed distributions. However, the relative efficiency of the quantile regression estimator compared to the OLS estimator can be arbitrarily small based on their respective asymptotic variances. Zou &amp; Yuan 2008a proposed a solution to this issue through the composite quantile regression (CQR) method, whose loss function aggregates multiple quantile regression loss functions. Specifically, for d = 1 d=1 and any K ∈ ℕ K\in\mathbb{N} , the CQR estimator b ~ \tilde{b} is obtained by the following optimization problem ( q ^ 1 , … , q ^ K , b ~ ) = arg ​ min q 1 , … , q K ∈ ℝ , b ∈ ℝ d × p ∑ i = 1 n ∑ k = 1 K ρ τ k ( Y i − bX i − q k ) , \displaystyle(\hat{q}_{1},\ldots,\hat{q}_{K},\tilde{b})=\argmin_{q_{1},\ldots,q_{K}\in\mathbb{R},\ b\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times p}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sum_{k=1}^{K}\rho_{\tau_{k}}(Y_{i}-bX_{i}-q_{k}), (2) where ρ τ ​ ( t ) \rho_{\tau}(t) is the so-called check function defined as ρ τ ​ ( t ) = max ⁡ { t , 0 } + ( τ − 1 ) ​ t \rho_{\tau}(t)=\max\{t,0\}+(\tau-1)t for any t ∈ ℝ t\in\mathbb{R} , and τ k = k / ( K + 1 ) \tau_{k}=k/(K+1) . Zou &amp; Yuan 2008a showed that the CQR estimator can achieve at least 70 % 70\% relative efficiency compared to the OLS estimator even for Gaussian noise. However, when d ≥ 2 d\geq 2 , the CQR estimator b ~ \tilde{b} does not have a natural extension due to the lack of a proper definition for multivariate rank/quantile and the corresponding multivariate check function. One of the key contributions of this study is the development of a multiple-output composite quantile regression (MCQR) estimator. The definition of our proposed estimator is closely related to the concept of the Monge–Kantorovich (MK) ranks/quantiles, which are multivariate generalization of ranks and quantiles from the view of optimal transport developed by Chernozhukov et al. 2017a and Hallin et al. 2021a . Intuitively, the univariate cumulative distribution function (CDF) and the quantile function of any probability distribution P X P^{X} can be viewed as optimal transport maps between P X P^{X} and a reference distribution U ⁡ [ 0 , 1 ] U[0,1] . This perspective allows for a natural extension of ranks and quantiles to multivariate distributions. Compared to many previous extensions based on Tukey’s depth ( Tukey 1975a ) , MK-ranks/quantiles have several advantages, including the ability to capture more complex and possibly non-convex quantile contours and allowing for distribution-free inference in multivariate settings. Please refer to Hallin 2022a for a comprehensive introduction to the MK-ranks/quantiles. A crucial observation in constructing our MCQR estimation is that the univariate CQR loss function can be equivalently described as the Wasserstein product between the empirical distribution of the residuals ( Y i − b X i : i = 1 , … , n ) (Y_{i}-bX_{i}:i=1,\ldots,n) and the uniform distribution U ⁡ [ 0 , 1 ] U[0,1] . Here, the ‘Wasserstein product’ between two distributions P P and Q Q is the maximum of 𝔼 ⁡ ( X ​ Y ) \mathbb{E}(XY) over all couplings ( X , Y ) (X,Y) with marginal distributions X ∼ P X\sim P and Y ∼ Q Y\sim Q . When Q Q is viewed as a reference distribution, this optimal coupling is exactly the same as in MK-quantiles. See ( 4 ) for a formal definition and more detailed discussion. This alternative viewpoint allows us to circumvent the need of defining individual multivariate check functions and instead formulate the MCQR loss in terms of the MK-quantiles. It is worthwhile to note that while various previous studies in the literature have attempted to extend the concept of quantile regression to the multiple-output setting ( Hallin et al. 2010a ; Kong &amp; Mizera 2012a ; Hallin et al. 2015a ; Carlier et al. 2016a ; Barrio et al. 2022a ) , the majority have concentrated on estimating the quantile contours rather than focusing on the robust estimation of the regression coefficients. See Section 2 for a more detailed discussion of our proposed method. Then in Section 3 we investigate the theoretical guarantees of the MCQR estimator. We first prove the consistency result when the random noise is only assumed to have finite ℓ \ell -th moment for some ℓ &gt; 2 \ell&gt;2 (see Theomre 5 ). Then a faster convergence rate is established when we assume a noise distribution with a sub-Weibull tail (see Theorem 8 ). We highlight that the MCQR procedure represents an M-estimation problem incorporating the Wasserstein distance within its loss function, for which the empirical process theory tools used in traditional M-estimators are not directly applicable. To the best of our knowledge, Theorem 5 and Theorem 8 are the first results that establish the consistency and convergence rate of an M-estimation where the loss function involves the 2-Wasserstein distance. New theoretical tools were developed along the way, which we believe may be of independent interest in future research. Please refer to Section 3 for detailed descriptions of the Theorems and proof sketches. 1.1 Related works Various definitions of multiple-output quantile regression have been proposed in the past, including the depth-based directional method ( Hallin et al. 2010a ; Kong &amp; Mizera 2012a ; Hallin et al. 2015a ) , the M-quantile ( Koltchinskii 1997c ) , the spatial quantile ( Chaudhuri 1996c ; Chakraborty &amp; Chaudhuri 2014c ) , among others. As remarked above, unlike our work, all these approaches focus on estimating the quantile contours of the response variable. In addition, these definition of multivariate quantiles do not preserve the quintessential attributes of the univariate quantile, notably distribution-freeness and the Glivenko-Cantelli property ( Hallin et al. 2021a ) . Furthermore, their quantile contours are constrained to be convex, which hinders performance when data distribution exhibits non-convex level sets. In contrast, Chernozhukov et al. 2017a and Hallin et al. 2021a introduced a novel multivariate quantile/rank framework based on optimal transport. This framework adeptly captures level set non-convexities while retaining the distribution-freeness and the Glivenko-Cantelli property, hallmarks of the univariate rank/quantile ( Chernozhukov et al. 2017a ; Hallin et al. 2021a ) . Several applications in multivariate statistics have been established successfully ( Deb &amp; Sen 2021a ; Barrio et al. 2022a ; Hallin et al. 2023a ; Shi et al. 2024a ) . We refer to a comprehensive survey Hallin 2022a and references therein. Building upon this groundwork, Carlier et al. 2016a and Barrio et al. 2022a proposed two notions of multiple-output quantile regression, though concentrating primarily on the estimation of conditional quantile functions rather than the regression coefficients themselves. 1.2 Notation For n ∈ ℕ n\in\mathbb{N} , write [ n ] := { 1 , … , n } [n]:=\{1,\ldots,n\} . We write ℬ \mathcal{B} as the Borel σ \sigma -algebra of ℝ d \mathbb{R}^{d} . Write 𝒫 ℓ ​ ( ℝ d ) \mathcal{P}_{\ell}(\mathbb{R}^{d}) as the set of Borel probability measures defined on ( ℝ d , ℬ ) (\mathbb{R}^{d},\mathcal{B}) with finite ℓ \ell -th order moments for ℓ ∈ ℕ \ell\in\mathbb{N} and 𝒫 a ​ c ​ ( ℝ d ) \mathcal{P}_{ac}(\mathbb{R}^{d}) be the set of probability measures on the same space that are absolutely continuous with respect to the Lebesgue measure. For any random variable X X on ℝ d \mathbb{R}^{d} , write P X P^{X} for the associated probability measure and P n X := 1 n ​ ∑ i = 1 n δ X i P_{n}^{X}:=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\delta_{X_{i}} for the associated empirical distribution where X 1 , … , X n X_{1},\ldots,X_{n} are n n independent copies of X X and δ x \delta_{x} denote the Dirac measure on x x . 2 The MCQR construction In this section, we present a generalization of the traditional CQR when the dimension of the response variable d d is greater than 1 1 . We start by revisiting the univariate CQR estimator, and showing that at the population level, it can be seen as the minimizer of the Wasserstein product between P Y − b ​ X P^{Y-bX} and the uniform reference distribution U ⁡ [ 0 , 1 ] U[0,1] , which allows a multivariate generalization. Moreover, we justify that the choice of the reference distribution does not affect the population minimizer in this problem, thus allowing us to select more natural reference distributions in multivariate settings. 2.1 Univariate CQR revisited Since q 1 , … , q K q_{1},\ldots,q_{K} in ( 2 ) have the interpretation of quantiles associated with τ 1 , … , τ K \tau_{1},\ldots,\tau_{K} , it is natural to further constrain the optimization by assuming q 1 ≤ ⋯ ≤ q K q_{1}\leq\cdots\leq q_{K} . Let ℳ \mathcal{M} denote the set of all increasing functions on ℝ \mathbb{R} , then ( 2 ) with this additional constraint can be viewed as the empirical version of the following optimization problem arg ​ min q ∈ ℳ , b ∈ ℝ 1 × p ⁡ 𝔼 ​ { ρ T ​ ( Y − bX − q ⁡ ( T ) ) } = arg ​ min q ∈ ℳ , b ∈ ℝ 1 × p ⁡ 𝔼 ​ { ∫ 0 1 ρ τ ​ ( Y − bX − q ⁡ ( τ ) ) ​ 𝑑 τ } , \argmin\limits_{q\in\mathcal{M},b\in\mathbb{R}^{1\times p}}\mathbb{E}\Bigl\{\rho_{T}\bigl(Y-bX-q(T)\bigr)\Bigr\}=\argmin\limits_{q\in\mathcal{M},b\in\mathbb{R}^{1\times p}}\mathbb{E}\Bigl\{\int_{0}^{1}\rho_{\tau}\bigl(Y-bX-q(\tau)\bigr)d\tau\Bigr\}, (3) where ( X , Y ) ∼ P ( X , Y ) (X,Y)\sim P^{(X,Y)} and T ∼ U ⁡ [ 0 , 1 ] T\sim U[0,1] . The following lemma indicates that, when d = 1 d=1 , the true regression coefficient b ∗ b^{*} in ( 1 ) and the quantile function q ε ∗ : τ ↦ inf { y ∈ ℝ : P ε ( − ∞ , y ] ≥ τ } q^{*}_{\varepsilon}:\tau\mapsto\inf\{y\in\mathbb{R}:P^{\varepsilon}(-\infty,y]\geq\tau\} of ε \varepsilon form a solution of ( 3 ). As we will see from Lemma 2 and Proposition 3 , this is actually the unique solution to the problem. Lemma 1 . Under the linear model ( 1 ), we have ( b ∗ , q ε ∗ ) ∈ arg ​ min b ∈ ℝ 1 × p , q ∈ ℳ ​ 𝔼 ​ ∫ 0 1 ρ τ ​ ( Y − bX − q ⁡ ( τ ) ) ​ 𝑑 τ . (b^{*},q_{\varepsilon}^{*})\in\argmin_{b\in\mathbb{R}^{1\times p},q\in\mathcal{M}}\E\int_{0}^{1}\rho_{\tau}(Y-bX-q(\tau))\,d\tau. In fact, an inspection of the proof (see Appendix A.3 ) of the above lemma reveals that if τ 1 , … , τ K \tau_{1},\ldots,\tau_{K} converges to a distribution P Z P^{Z} with support 𝒵 \mathcal{Z} rather than to U ⁡ [ 0 , 1 ] U[0,1] , then a similar result to Lemma 1 holds provided that we modify the convex check functions ρ τ : ℝ → ℝ + \rho_{\tau}:\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}^{+} for τ ∈ 𝒵 \tau\in\mathcal{Z} so that they satisfy F W − 1 ∘ F Z ​ ( τ ) ∈ arg ​ min θ ​ 𝔼 ​ ρ τ ​ ( W − θ ) F_{W}^{-1}\circ F_{Z}(\tau)\in\argmin_{\theta}\E\rho_{\tau}(W-\theta) for all random variables W W with absolutely continuous distributions. However, generalizing the check functions beyond the univariate setting is difficult. While some attempts have been made ( Chaudhuri 1996c ; Koltchinskii 1997c ) , the resulting multivariate quantiles, defined through the minimizer of these generalized check functions, lack key properties of their univariate counterparts (see our discussion in Section 1.1 , as well as empirical comparisons in Section 4 ). Instead, our work takes a different approach and generalizes the CQR population loss function as a whole rather than individual check functions. A key observation that allows us to achieve this is the following reformulation of the loss function of ( 3 ) in Lemma 2 below. To state the lemma, we define the Wasserstein product between P , Q ∈ 𝒫 2 ​ ( ℝ d ) P,Q\in\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d}) as ⟨ ⟨ P , Q ⟩ ⟩ 𝒲 2 := sup γ ∈ 𝒞 ⁡ ( P , Q ) ∫ ⟨ x , y ⟩ ​ 𝑑 γ ​ ( x , y ) , \displaystyle\langle\!\langle P,Q\rangle\!\rangle_{\mathcal{W}_{2}}:=\sup_{\gamma\in\mathcal{C}(P,Q)}\int\langle x,y\rangle d\gamma(x,y), (4) where 𝒞 ⁡ ( P , Q ) \mathcal{C}(P,Q) denotes the set of all couplings between P P and Q Q , i.e. ​for any γ ∈ 𝒞 ⁡ ( P , Q ) \gamma\in\mathcal{C}(P,Q) , and measureable subsets A A , B ⊂ ℝ d B\subset\mathbb{R}^{d} , we have γ ⁡ ( A × ℝ d ) = P ⁡ ( A ) \gamma(A\times\mathbb{R}^{d})=P(A) and γ ⁡ ( ℝ d × B ) = Q ⁡ ( B ) \gamma(\mathbb{R}^{d}\times B)=Q(B) . The name ‘Wasserstein product’ stems from its intrinsic link with the 2-Wasserstein distance: 1 2 ​ 𝒲 2 2 ​ ( P , Q ) = 1 2 ​ ∫ ‖ x ‖ 2 ​ 𝑑 P ​ ( x ) + 1 2 ​ ∫ ‖ y ‖ 2 ​ 𝑑 Q ​ ( y ) − ⟨ ⟨ P , Q ⟩ ⟩ 𝒲 2 \frac{1}{2}\mathcal{W}_{2}^{2}(P,Q)=\frac{1}{2}\int\|x\|^{2}\,dP(x)+\frac{1}{2}\int\|y\|^{2}dQ(y)-\langle\!\langle P,Q\rangle\!\rangle_{\mathcal{W}_{2}} . We will often slightly abuse notation to write ⟨ ⟨ X , Y ⟩ ⟩ 𝒲 2 \langle\!\langle X,Y\rangle\!\rangle_{\mathcal{W}_{2}} instead of ⟨ ⟨ P X , P Y ⟩ ⟩ 𝒲 2 \langle\!\langle P^{X},P^{Y}\rangle\!\rangle_{\mathcal{W}_{2}} . Lemma 2 . Suppose that X ∼ P X X\sim P^{X} is mean-zero with finite second moments. For U ∼ U ⁡ [ 0 , 1 ] U\sim U[0,1] , and a fixed b ∈ ℝ 1 × p b\in\mathbb{R}^{1\times p} , we have inf q ∈ ℳ 𝔼 ⁡ { ∫ 0 1 ρ τ ​ ( Y − b ​ X − q ⁡ ( τ ) ) ​ 𝑑 τ } + 1 2 ​ 𝔼 ⁡ Y = ⟨ ⟨ Y − bX , U ⟩ ⟩ 𝒲 2 . \inf_{q\in\mathcal{M}}\mathbb{E}\Bigl\{\int_{0}^{1}\rho_{\tau}\bigl(Y-bX-q(\tau)\bigr)d\tau\Bigr\}+\frac{1}{2}\E Y=\langle\!\langle Y-bX,U\rangle\!\rangle_{\mathcal{W}_{2}}. The proof is deferred to Appendix A.4 . Writing ℒ ⁡ ( b , U ) := ⟨ ⟨ Y − b ​ X , U ⟩ ⟩ 𝒲 2 \mathcal{L}(b;U):=\langle\!\langle Y-bX,U\rangle\!\rangle_{\mathcal{W}_{2}} , Lemma 2 and Equation ( 3 ) imply that, the optimizer in b b for the population CQR loss function in ( 3 ) is equal to arg ​ min b ∈ ℝ d × p ⁡ ℒ ​ ( b , U ) \argmin_{b\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times p}}\mathcal{L}(b;U) when d = 1 d=1 . 2.2 Multiple-output CQR via optimal transport With the help of Lemma 2 , we may regard ℒ ⁡ ( b , U ) \mathcal{L}(b;U) as a generalized population CQR loss function for the multiple-output case ( d ≥ 2 d\geq 2 ) for suitably chosen reference random vector U U . The following proposition (see Appendix A.5 for proof) verifies that under a mild condition this loss has a unique minimizer and that is independent of the specific choice of U U (see Appendix C for an intuitive illustration). Proposition 3 . If P ε , P U ∈ 𝒫 2 ​ ( ℝ d ) ∩ 𝒫 a ​ c ​ ( ℝ d ) P^{\varepsilon},P^{U}\in\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\cap\mathcal{P}_{ac}(\mathbb{R}^{d}) and P X P^{X} is not a point mass, then b ∗ b^{*} is the unique minimizer of ℒ ⁡ ( b , U ) \mathcal{L}(b;U) . There are various choices of the reference distribution of U U , including the uniform distribution on the unit cube ( Chernozhukov et al. 2017a ; Deb &amp; Sen 2021a ) and the spherical uniform distribution ( Hallin et al. 2021a ; Barrio et al. 2022a ) . In this paper, we opt for the standard multivariate normal distribution as the reference distribution, primarily motivated by its advantageous theoretical characteristics. Moreover, we will also omit the specification of the reference distribution in the loss function and simply write it as ℒ ⁡ ( b ) \mathcal{L}(b) throughout the rest of the paper. Proposition 3 motivates the following natural estimator of b ∗ b^{*} based on the Wasserstein product of the empirical distributions. Definition 4 . Given i.i.d. covariate-response pairs ( X 1 , Y 1 ) , … , ( X n , Y n ) (X_{1},Y_{1}),\ldots,(X_{n},Y_{n}) generated as in ( 1 ) and a reference distribution P U ∈ 𝒫 2 ​ ( ℝ d ) ∩ 𝒫 a ​ c ​ ( ℝ d ) P^{U}\in\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\cap\mathcal{P}_{ac}(\mathbb{R}^{d}) and U 1 , … , U m ∼ i . i . d . P U U_{1},\ldots,U_{m}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle i.i.d.}}{{\sim}}P^{U} , the MCQR estimator for b ∗ b^{*} is defined as b ^ ∈ arg ​ min b ∈ ℝ d × p ℒ n , m ( b ) , where ℒ n , m ​ ( b ) := ⟨ ⟨ P n Y − b ​ X , P m U ⟩ ⟩ 𝒲 2 . \displaystyle\hat{b}\in\argmin_{b\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times p}}\mathcal{L}_{n,m}(b),\ \text{where $\mathcal{L}_{n,m}(b):=\langle\!\langle P_{n}^{Y-bX},P_{m}^{U}\rangle\!\rangle_{\mathcal{W}_{2}}$}. (5) The optimization procedure above is an M-estimation problem. However, unlike classical M-estimation problems, the empirical loss function cannot be viewed as an empirical process of the population loss (in fact, 𝔼 ​ ⟨ ⟨ P n Y − b ​ X , P m U ⟩ ⟩ 𝒲 2 ≠ ⟨ ⟨ P Y − b ​ X , P U ⟩ ⟩ 𝒲 2 \mathbb{E}\langle\!\langle P_{n}^{Y-bX},P_{m}^{U}\rangle\!\rangle_{\mathcal{W}_{2}}\neq\langle\!\langle P^{Y-bX},P^{U}\rangle\!\rangle_{\mathcal{W}_{2}} ), which prevents us from applying traditional empirical process theory techniques to obtain the convergence rate results directly. Instead, a collection of new theoretical results is developed to better understand both the population and empirical version of the Wasserstein product loss. Please refer to Section 3 for more details. Secondly, it is worth noting that the empirical reference distribution P m U P_{m}^{U} is distinct from the distribution of τ k \tau_{k} ’s in ( 2 ) when d = 1 d=1 . Instead, we employ it as the reference distribution to redefine the distribution function and the quantile function (refer to Appendix C for an example). Thus, even when d = 1 d=1 with a uniform reference distribution, the plug-in estimator in ( 5 ) does not reduce to the univariate CQR estimator ( 2 ). This can also be seen from the proof of Lemma 2 . Therefore, our proposed MCQR estimator ( 5 ) is different from the univariate CQR estimator that is studied in Zou &amp; Yuan 2008a but shares the same loss function at the population level. See also Figure 3(a) and Figure 3(b) for an interesting difference in their robustness to contamination in one dimension. 2.3 Solving MCQR via linear programming We describe here how the optimization problem can be solved in practice. Given { ( X i , Y i ) } i = 1 n ⊂ ℝ p × ℝ d \{(X_{i},Y_{i})\}_{i=1}^{n}\subset\mathbb{R}^{p}\times\mathbb{R}^{d} and { U i } i = 1 m \{U_{i}\}_{i=1}^{m} , we define X = ( X 1 , … , X n ) ⊤ ∈ ℝ n × p {X}=(X_{1},\ldots,X_{n})^{\top}\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times p} and Y = ( Y 1 , … , Y n ) ⊤ ∈ ℝ n × d Y=(Y_{1},\ldots,Y_{n})^{\top}\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times d} and U = ( U 1 , … , U m ) ⊤ ∈ ℝ m × d U=(U_{1},\ldots,U_{m})^{\top}\in\mathbb{R}^{m\times d} . Define 𝒞 n , m = { A ∈ ℝ + m × n : A ​ 𝟏 n = 𝟏 m / m ​ and ​ A ⊤ ​ 𝟏 m = 𝟏 n / n } . \mathcal{C}_{n,m}=\{A\in\mathbb{R}_{+}^{m\times n}:A\mathbf{1}_{n}=\mathbf{1}_{m}/m\text{ and }A^{\top}\mathbf{1}_{m}=\mathbf{1}_{n}/n\}. Every π ∈ 𝒞 n , m \pi\in\mathcal{C}_{n,m} represents a coupling of P n ( X , Y ) P^{(X,Y)}_{n} and P m U P^{U}_{m} in the sense that π i , j \pi_{i,j} denotes the mass to be transported from ( X i , Y i ) (X_{i},Y_{i}) to U j U_{j} . Then by the definition of ⟨ ⟨ ⋅ , ⋅ ⟩ ⟩ 𝒲 2 \langle\!\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle\!\rangle_{\mathcal{W}_{2}} , the optimization problem in ( 5 ) can be written as min b ∈ ℝ d × p ⁡ max π ∈ 𝒞 n , m ​ Tr ⁡ ( U ⊤ ​ π ​ ( Y − Xb ⊤ ) ) \displaystyle\min_{b\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times p}}\max\limits_{\pi\in\mathcal{C}_{n,m}}\Tr\bigl(U^{\top}\pi(Y-Xb^{\top})\bigr) = max π ∈ 𝒞 n , m ⁡ min b ∈ ℝ d × p ​ Tr ⁡ ( U ⊤ ​ π ​ ( Y − Xb ⊤ ) ) \displaystyle=\max\limits_{\pi\in\mathcal{C}_{n,m}}\min_{b\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times p}}\Tr\bigl(U^{\top}\pi(Y-Xb^{\top})\bigr) = max π ∈ 𝒞 n , m ⁡ min b ∈ ℝ d × p ​ { Tr ⁡ ( U ⊤ ​ π ​ Y ) − Tr ⁡ ( U ⊤ ​ π ​ Xb ⊤ ) } , \displaystyle=\max_{\pi\in\mathcal{C}_{n,m}}\min\limits_{b\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times p}}\bigl\{\Tr(U^{\top}\pi Y)-\Tr(U^{\top}\pi Xb^{\top})\bigr\}, where the exchange of the minimum and maximum is allowed as the objective is linear ( Neumann 1928a ) . The dual formulation on the right-hand side is easier to handle since its inner minimum is equal to − ∞ -\infty unless U ⊤ ​ π ​ X = 0 U^{\top}\pi X=0 . Hence, the dual problem of ( 5 ) is max π ∈ 𝒞 n , m \displaystyle\max_{\pi\in\mathcal{C}_{n,m}} Tr ⁡ ( U ⊤ ​ π ​ Y ) \displaystyle\quad\Tr(U^{\top}\pi Y) s . t . \displaystyle\mathrm{s.t.} U ⊤ ​ π ​ X = 0 , \displaystyle\quad U^{\top}\pi X=0, which can be solved by standard linear programming solvers. After obtaining the dual optimizer π ^ \hat{\pi} , the MCQR estimator b ^ \hat{b} is obtained via complementary slackness. 3 Theoretical guarantees In this section, we investigate the theoretical performance of the proposed estimator when adopting a standard Gaussian reference distribution U ∼ 𝒩 ⁡ ( 0 , I d ) U\sim\mathcal{N}(0,I_{d}) . In Theorem 5 , we provide a non-asymptotic bound for the estimation error when only assuming a finite 2 + δ 2+\delta moment condition on the random noise term. Furthermore, we demonstrate in Theorem 8 that in cases where the distributions of both the covariates and the noise exhibit a sub-Weibull tail, the MCQR estimator enjoys a faster rate of convergence to the truth. Given a positive definite matrix Σ ∈ ℝ p × p \Sigma\in\mathbb{R}^{p\times p} and any matrix A ∈ ℝ d × p A\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times p} , we define the matrix Mahalanobis norm of A A with respect to Σ \Sigma as ‖ A ‖ Σ := Tr 1 / 2 ⁡ ( A ​ Σ ​ A ⊤ ) = ‖ A ​ Σ 1 / 2 ‖ F \|A\|_{\Sigma}:=\Tr^{1/2}(A\Sigma A^{\top})=\|A\Sigma^{1/2}\|_{\mathrm{F}} . We will assume throughout this section that 𝔼 ⁡ ( XX ⊤ ) = Σ \E(XX^{\top})=\Sigma . Assumption 1 X X follows an elliptical distribution, i.e., there exists independent random variable R R on ℝ + \mathbb{R}_{+} and random vector Q ∼ U ⁡ ( 𝒮 d − 1 ) Q\sim U(\mathcal{S}^{d-1}) such that X = Σ 1 / 2 ​ Q ​ R X=\Sigma^{1/2}QR . Under this assumption on X X , we first consider the case when the random noise ε \varepsilon is only assumed to satisfy a finite moment condition. Theorem 5 . Suppose ( X , Y ) , ( X 1 , Y 1 ) , … , ( X n , Y n ) (X,Y),(X_{1},Y_{1}),\ldots,(X_{n},Y_{n}) are i.i.d. pairs generated according to ( 1 ), U 1 , … , U m ∼ i . i . d . 𝒩 ⁡ ( 0 , I d ) U_{1},\ldots,U_{m}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathrm{i.i.d.}}}{{\sim}}\mathcal{N}(0,I_{d}) . Assume m ≥ n &gt; 1 m\geq n&gt;1 and that Assumption 3 holds. If P X , P ε ∈ 𝒫 ℓ ​ ( ℝ d ) P^{X},P^{\varepsilon}\in\mathcal{P}_{\ell}(\mathbb{R}^{d}) for ℓ &gt; 2 \ell&gt;2 then there exists C &gt; 0 C&gt;0 depending only on ℓ , d \ell,d and p p such that with probability at least 1 − 4 ​ ( log ⁡ n ) − 1 1-4(\log n)^{-1} , the MCQR estimator defined in ( 5 ) satisfies ‖ b ^ − b ∗ ‖ Σ 2 ∧ 1 ≤ C ⁡ ( n − 1 4 + n − 1 d ∨ p + n 2 − ℓ 2 ​ ℓ ) ​ log ⁡ m . \|\hat{b}-b^{*}\|_{\Sigma}^{2}\wedge 1\leq C\bigl(n^{-\frac{1}{4}}+n^{-\frac{1}{d\vee p}}+n^{\frac{2-\ell}{2\ell}}\bigr)\log m. An immediate consequence of Theorem 5 is that if taking n n and m m to be large enough such that C ⁡ ( n − 1 4 + n − 1 d ∨ p + n 2 − ℓ 2 ​ ℓ ) ​ log ⁡ m &lt; 1 , \displaystyle C\bigl(n^{-\frac{1}{4}}+n^{-\frac{1}{d\vee p}}+n^{\frac{2-\ell}{2\ell}}\bigr)\log m&lt;1, (6) then we have ‖ b ^ − b ∗ ‖ Σ 2 ≤ C ⁡ ( n − 1 4 + n − 1 d ∨ p + n 2 − ℓ 2 ​ ℓ ) ​ log ⁡ m \displaystyle\|\hat{b}-b^{*}\|_{\Sigma}^{2}\leq C\bigl(n^{-\frac{1}{4}}+n^{-\frac{1}{d\vee p}}+n^{\frac{2-\ell}{2\ell}}\bigr)\log m (7) holds with probability at least 1 − 4 ​ ( log ⁡ n ) − 1 1-4(\log n)^{-1} . We make a few remarks here. Firstly, to the best of our knowledge, this is the first consistency result for an M-estimator whose loss function involves a multivariate 2-Wasserstein distance term. Bernton et al. 2019a studied the convergence rate and asymptotic distribution of a minimum Wasserstein estimator, but their result is restricted to 1-Wasserstein distance in the univariate setting, for which explicit characterization of the optimal transport is available. In our setting, the traditional M-estimator/Z-estimator argument ( Vaart &amp; Wellner 1996a , Chapter 3.2-3.3) that derives consistency and rate of convergence of an M-estimator by analyzing the curvature of the loss function is infeasible. Instead, our proof relies on several new lemmas that reveal important properties of the Wasserstein product. To briefly sketch the proof of Theorem 5 , we first introduce the following lemmas. Lemma 6 . Let Z Z and ε \varepsilon be independent random vectors in ℝ d \mathbb{R}^{d} and U ∼ 𝒩 ⁡ ( 0 , I d ) U\sim\mathcal{N}(0,I_{d}) . If P ε P^{\varepsilon} and P Z P^{Z} are atomless probability measures with finite-second moments, then ⟨ ⟨ Z + ε , U ⟩ ⟩ 𝒲 2 2 ≥ ⟨ ⟨ Z , U ⟩ ⟩ 𝒲 2 2 + ⟨ ⟨ ε , U ⟩ ⟩ 𝒲 2 2 . \langle\!\langle Z+\varepsilon,U\rangle\!\rangle_{\mathcal{W}_{2}}^{2}\geq\langle\!\langle Z,U\rangle\!\rangle_{\mathcal{W}_{2}}^{2}+\langle\!\langle\varepsilon,U\rangle\!\rangle_{\mathcal{W}_{2}}^{2}. This lemma is proved by constructing a sequence of couplings of the triple ( Z , ε , U ) (Z,\varepsilon,U) via the Slepian smart path interpolation (see e.g. Vershynin 2018a , Chapter 7.2.1) . The best induced coupling of ( Z + ε , U ) (Z+\varepsilon,U) provides the desired lower bound of ⟨ ⟨ Z + ε , U ⟩ ⟩ 𝒲 2 \langle\!\langle Z+\varepsilon,U\rangle\!\rangle_{\mathcal{W}_{2}} . See Appendix A.7 for the proof. We remark that the lower bound in Lemma 6 is sharp, as can be seen from Lemma 23 in Appendix B . Lemma 7 . Let X 1 X_{1} , X 2 X_{2} , Y 1 Y_{1} , Y 2 Y_{2} be random elements taking values in a common normed space 𝒳 \mathcal{X} . We have | ⟨ ⟨ X 1 , X 2 ⟩ ⟩ 𝒲 2 − ⟨ ⟨ Y 1 , Y 2 ⟩ ⟩ 𝒲 2 | ≤ ( 𝔼 ⁡ ‖ Y 2 ‖ 2 ) 1 / 2 ​ 𝒲 2 ​ ( P X 1 , P Y 1 ) + ( 𝔼 ⁡ ‖ X 1 ‖ 2 ) 1 / 2 ​ 𝒲 2 ​ ( P X 2 , P Y 2 ) . \bigl|\langle\!\langle X_{1},X_{2}\rangle\!\rangle_{\mathcal{W}_{2}}-\langle\!\langle Y_{1},Y_{2}\rangle\!\rangle_{\mathcal{W}_{2}}\bigr|\leq\bigl(\E\|Y_{2}\|^{2}\bigr)^{1/2}\mathcal{W}_{2}(P^{X_{1}},P^{Y_{1}})+\bigl(\E\|X_{1}\|^{2}\bigr)^{1/2}\mathcal{W}_{2}(P^{X_{2}},P^{Y_{2}}). This lemma links 𝒲 2 ​ ( P X 1 , P X 2 ) \mathcal{W}_{2}(P^{X_{1}},P^{X_{2}}) , 𝒲 2 ​ ( P Y 1 , P Y 2 ) \mathcal{W}_{2}(P^{Y_{1}},P^{Y_{2}}) with 𝒲 2 ​ ( P X 1 , P Y 1 ) \mathcal{W}_{2}(P^{X_{1}},P^{Y_{1}}) , 𝒲 2 ​ ( P X 2 , P Y 2 ) \mathcal{W}_{2}(P^{X_{2}},P^{Y_{2}}) . This is useful when transforming a two-sample problem into two one-sample problems. Please refer to Appendix A.8 for the proof. (a) The upper and lower bound constructed in the Proof of Theorem 5 . (b) The upper and lower bound constructed in the proof of Theorem 8 . Figure 1: Illustration of proofs. Proof Sketch of Theorem 5 We start with the basic inequality: ℒ ⁡ ( b ^ ) − ℒ ⁡ ( b ∗ ) ≤ ℒ ⁡ ( b ^ ) − ℒ n , m ​ ( b ^ ) + ℒ n , m ​ ( b ∗ ) − ℒ ⁡ ( b ∗ ) . \displaystyle\mathcal{L}(\hat{{b}})-\mathcal{L}({{b}^{*}})\leq\mathcal{L}(\hat{{b}})-\mathcal{L}_{n,m}(\hat{{b}})+\mathcal{L}_{n,m}({{b}^{*}})-\mathcal{L}({{b}^{*}}). (8) The proof strategy involves establishing a lower bound for the left-hand side of ( 8 ) with respect to ‖ b ^ − b ∗ ‖ Σ \|\hat{b}-b^{*}\|_{\Sigma} and an upper bound for the right-hand side of ( 8 ) in terms of ‖ b ^ − b ∗ ‖ Σ \|\hat{b}-b^{*}\|_{\Sigma} . Then by solving the resulting inequality, we can derive an expression bounding ‖ b ^ − b ∗ ‖ Σ \|\hat{b}-b^{*}\|_{\Sigma} . For a lower bound of the left-hand side of ( 8 ), since for any b ∈ ℝ d × p b\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times p} , we have ℒ ⁡ ( b ) − ℒ ⁡ ( b ∗ ) = ⟨ ⟨ ( b ∗ − b ) ​ X + ε , U ⟩ ⟩ 𝒲 2 − ⟨ ⟨ ε , U ⟩ ⟩ 𝒲 2 \mathcal{L}({b})-\mathcal{L}({{b}^{*}})=\langle\!\langle{(b^{*}-b)X+\varepsilon},{U}\rangle\!\rangle_{\mathcal{W}_{2}}-\langle\!\langle{\varepsilon},{U}\rangle\!\rangle_{\mathcal{W}_{2}} , by applying Lemma 6 and the explicit form for ⟨ ⟨ ( b ∗ − b ) ​ X , U ⟩ ⟩ 𝒲 2 \langle\!\langle(b^{*}-b)X,U\rangle\!\rangle_{\mathcal{W}_{2}} we can show that ℒ ⁡ ( b ) − ℒ ⁡ ( b ∗ ) ≥ r 2 + ‖ b ∗ − b ‖ Σ 2 − r , \displaystyle\mathcal{L}(b)-\mathcal{L}(b^{*})\geq\sqrt{r^{2}+\|b^{*}-b\|_{\Sigma}^{2}}-r, (9) where r := ⟨ ⟨ ε , U ⟩ ⟩ 𝒲 2 r:=\langle\!\langle\varepsilon,U\rangle\!\rangle_{\mathcal{W}_{2}} is a constant. This lower bound grows quadratically in ‖ b ^ − b ∗ ‖ Σ \|\hat{b}-b^{*}\|_{\Sigma} when ‖ b ^ − b ∗ ‖ Σ \|\hat{b}-b^{*}\|_{\Sigma} is close to zero and linearly when ‖ b ^ − b ∗ ‖ Σ \|\hat{b}-b^{*}\|_{\Sigma} is large (see Figure 1(a) for an illustration). To upper bound the right-hand side of ( 8 ), by applying Lemma 7 we have for each b ∈ ℝ d × p b\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times p} , | ℒ ( b ) − ℒ n , m ( b ) | ≤ ( 1 m ∑ i = 1 m \displaystyle|\mathcal{L}(b)-\mathcal{L}_{n,m}(b)|\leq\biggl(\frac{1}{m}\sum_{i=1}^{m} OPEN ‖ U i ‖ 2 ) 1 / 2 ​ 𝒲 2 ​ ( P Y − b ​ X , P n Y − b ​ X ) \displaystyle\|U_{i}\|^{2}\biggr)^{1/2}\mathcal{W}_{2}(P^{Y-bX},P_{n}^{Y-bX}) + ( 𝔼 ⁡ ‖ Y − bX ‖ 2 ) 1 / 2 ​ 𝒲 2 ​ ( P U , P m U ) . \displaystyle+(\E\|Y-bX\|^{2})^{1/2}\mathcal{W}_{2}(P^{U},P_{m}^{U}). (10) Here 𝒲 2 ​ ( P Y − b ​ X , P n Y − b ​ X ) \mathcal{W}_{2}(P^{Y-bX},P_{n}^{Y-bX}) and 𝒲 2 ​ ( P U , P m U ) \mathcal{W}_{2}(P^{U},P_{m}^{U}) are one-sample empirical Wasserstein distance, and the state-of-art convergence rate can be applied (see e.g. Fournier &amp; Guillin 2015c ) (the actual proof is more involved in the sense that we need to establish the same result uniformly over b b ). Then a direct calculation on the right-hand side of ( 10 ) leads to a quadratic upper bound in terms of ‖ b ∗ − b ‖ Σ \|b^{*}-b\|_{\Sigma} . The result follows by combining the upper bound with the lower bound ( 9 ). See Appendix A.6 for a complete proof. Before we state a faster convergence rate result, we first introduce the following assumptions. Assumption 2 For some σ 1 , σ 2 &gt; 0 \sigma_{1},\sigma_{2}&gt;0 and α , β ∈ ( 0 , 2 ] \alpha,\beta\in(0,2] , it holds that the distribution of Σ − 1 / 2 X \Sigma^{-1/2}X is ( σ 1 , α ) ​ -sub-Weibull (\sigma_{1},\alpha)\textup{-sub-Weibull} and P ε P^{\varepsilon} is ( σ 2 , β ) ​ -sub-Weibull (\sigma_{2},\beta)\textup{-sub-Weibull} , in the sense that 𝔼 exp { 1 2 ( ∥ Σ − 1 / 2 X ∥ / σ 1 ) α } ≤ 2 and 𝔼 exp { 1 2 ( ∥ ε ∥ / σ 2 ) β } ≤ 2 \displaystyle\E\exp\biggl\{{\frac{1}{2}(\|\Sigma^{-1/2}X\|/\sigma_{1}})^{\alpha}\biggr\}\leq 2\quad\text{and}\quad\E\exp\biggl\{{\frac{1}{2}(\|\varepsilon\|/\sigma_{2}})^{\beta}\biggr\}\leq 2 (11) Assumption 3 Suppose P ε ∈ 𝒫 a ​ c ​ ( ℝ d ) P^{\varepsilon}\in\mathcal{P}_{ac}(\mathbb{R}^{d}) . For some γ 1 , γ 2 &gt; 0 \gamma_{1},\gamma_{2}&gt;0 , the density function of ε \varepsilon , write as f ε f_{\varepsilon} , satisfies the following anti-concentration property f ε ​ ( e ) ≥ γ 1 ​ exp ⁡ ( − γ 2 ​ ‖ e ‖ 2 ) , for ‖ e ‖ ≥ 1 . \displaystyle f_{\varepsilon}(e)\geq\gamma_{1}\exp{(-\gamma_{2}\|e\|^{2})},\quad\text{for $\|e\|\geq 1$}. (12) On the one hand, Assumption 3 immediately implies the following anti-concentration bound ℙ ⁡ ( ‖ ε ‖ ≥ r ) ≥ π d / 2 ​ ( ( r + 1 ) d − r d ) Γ ⁡ ( d 2 + 1 ) ​ γ 1 ​ exp ⁡ ( − 2 ​ γ 2 ​ r 2 − 2 ​ γ 2 ) , for r ≥ 1 . \displaystyle\Prob(\|\varepsilon\|\geq r)\geq\frac{\pi^{d/2}\bigl((r+1)^{d}-r^{d}\bigr)}{\Gamma(\frac{d}{2}+1)}\gamma_{1}\exp(-2\gamma_{2}r^{2}-2\gamma_{2}),\quad\text{for $r\geq 1$}. This indicates that the random noise ε \varepsilon possesses a heavier tail than the sub-gaussian tail outside the unit ball. On the other hand, by proposition 24 (i) , the sub-Weibull assumption implies that ℙ ⁡ ( ‖ ε ‖ ≥ r ) ≤ 2 ​ e − 1 2 ​ ( r / σ 2 ) β \Prob(\|\varepsilon\|\geq r)\leq 2e^{-\frac{1}{2}(r/\sigma_{2})^{\beta}} . The anti-concentration condition in ( 12 ) is a relaxation of the so-called ( γ 1 , γ 2 ) (\gamma_{1},\gamma_{2}) -regularity defined in Polyanskiy &amp; Wu 2016a . The merit of employing this relaxation becomes apparent when examining Lemma 27 , where it is demonstrated that the convolution of two independent probability densities adhering to ( 12 ) continues to satisfy the anti-concentration inequality. In contrast, the convolution of two independent regular densities may not be regular. Equipped with these assumptions, we are ready to state an improved convergence rate. Theorem 8 . Under the same setup of Theorem 5 and suppose that Assumptions 3 and 3 are satisfied. For m , n m,n large enough such that ( 6 ) is satisfied, there exists some constant M &gt; 0 M&gt;0 depending only on d , α , β , σ 1 , σ 2 , γ 1 , γ 2 d,\alpha,\beta,\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2},\gamma_{1},\gamma_{2} such that with probability at least 1 − 33 ​ ( log ⁡ n ) − 1 1-33(\log n)^{-1} , we have ∥ b ∗ − b ^ ∥ Σ 2 ≤ M ( ( p / n ) 1 / 2 + n − 2 / d ) ( log m ) 8 2 ∧ α ∧ β . \|b^{*}-\hat{b}\|_{\Sigma}^{2}\leq M\bigl((p/n)^{1/2}+n^{-2/d}\bigr)(\log m)^{\frac{8}{2\wedge\alpha\wedge\beta}}. (13) When d &gt; 4 d&gt;4 , up to a factor of the logarithm, the empirical Wasserstein distance estimation error n − 2 / d n^{-2/d} is the dominant term. This is derived from a uniform empirical Wasserstein distance control (see ( 14 ) and Proposition 17 ), and its minimax optimality has been established in Singh &amp; Póczos 2018a . Compared to ( 7 ), this improved bound in ( 13 ) removes the dependence on p p in the exponent. Moreover, unlike the convergence rate result established for the projected Wasserstein distance in Wang et al. 2021a ; Wang et al. 2022a , our argument does not require the distribution of ε \varepsilon to have compact support. When d ≤ 4 d\leq 4 , the parametric rate ( p / n ) 1 / 2 (p/n)^{1/2} dominates the estimation error. However, this does not translate into a the root- n n consistency even when d = 1 d=1 . We conjecture that this is likely due to an artifact of our proof. Specifically, due to a lack of effective tools to analyze the curvation of the loss function that incorporates the Wasserstein distance, we were unable to obtain concentration results for ∂ ∂ b ​ ( ℒ ⁡ ( b ) − ℒ n , m ​ ( b ) ) \frac{\partial}{\partial b}(\mathcal{L}(b)-\mathcal{L}_{n,m}(b)) uniformly over b b in a similar way that we have done for ℒ ​ ( b ) − ℒ n , m ​ ( b ) \mathcal{L}(b)-\mathcal{L}_{n,m}(b) . Exploration along this direction remains an area for future work. We briefly sketch the proof below. See Appendix A.9 for a complete proof. Proof Sketch of Theorem 8 Assume the setting of Theomem 5 , error bound ( 7 ) implies that on a high probability event, b ^ \hat{b} will lie in a bounded ball centered at b ∗ b^{*} , denoted by ℬ \mathcal{B} . Thus the basic inequality ( 8 ) indicates the following uniform bound ℒ ⁡ ( b ^ ) − ℒ ⁡ ( b ∗ ) \displaystyle\mathcal{L}(\hat{b})-\mathcal{L}(b^{*}) ≤ 2 ​ sup b ∈ ℬ | ℒ ⁡ ( b ) − ℒ n , m ​ ( b ) | \displaystyle\leq 2\sup_{b\in\mathcal{B}}|\mathcal{L}(b)-\mathcal{L}_{n,m}(b)| ≤ | 1 m ​ ∑ i = 1 m ‖ U i ‖ 2 − 𝔼 ⁡ ‖ U ‖ 2 | + sup b ∈ ℬ | 1 n ​ ∑ i = 1 n ‖ Y i − bX i ‖ 2 − 𝔼 ⁡ ‖ Y − bX ‖ 2 | \displaystyle\leq\Bigl|\frac{1}{m}\sum_{i=1}^{m}\|U_{i}\|^{2}-\E\|U\|^{2}\Bigr|+\sup_{b\in\mathcal{B}}\Bigl|\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\|Y_{i}-bX_{i}\|^{2}-\E\|Y-bX\|^{2}\Bigr| + sup b ∈ ℬ | 𝒲 2 2 ( P Y − b ​ X , P U ) − 𝒲 2 2 ( P n Y − b ​ X , P m U ) | . \displaystyle\quad+\sup_{b\in\mathcal{B}}\Bigl|\mathcal{W}_{2}^{2}(P^{Y-bX},P^{U})-\mathcal{W}_{2}^{2}(P_{n}^{Y-bX},P_{m}^{U})\Bigr|. (14) Utilizing the same lower bound for the left-hand side as in ( 9 ), it remains to derive an upper bound for the right-hand side of the above inequality. While the initial two terms of ( 14 ) can be effectively controlled through the application of statistical concentration arguments, as elucidated in Lemma 21 , achieving control over the last term demands much more effort. Motivated by the duality argument presented in Manole &amp; Niles-Weed 2024c , we establish a non-asymptotic uniform error bound for the empirical 2-Wasserstein distance (Proposition 17 in Appendix A.9 ; see also Figure 1(b) for an illustration), which forms the key ingredient of the proof. 4 Numerical experiments In this section, we compare the empirical performance of MCQR with other robust regression estimators. The MCQR estimator is obtained by solving the linear programming problem in Section 2.3 . The competitors used in the simulation studies include the ordinary least squares estimator (LS), the spatial quantile regression (SpQR) with zero quantile level ( Chaudhuri 1996c ) , and coordinate-wise CQR (CoorCQR), i.e. independently applying CQR to each component of the response variable. We refer readers to Appendix D for more details about SpQR. (a) Gaussian noise (b) multivariate t 2 t_{2} noise (c) Pareto copula noise (d) Banana-shaped noise Figure 2: Logarithmic average loss, measured in matrix Mahalanobis norm, of the regression coefficient estimated by MCQR, CoorCQR, SpQR and LS for data generated according to the mechanism described in Section 4 for various sample size n n , covariate dimension p p and response dimension d d and four different noise distributions (panels (a) to (d)). In each experiment, we draw i.i.d. data ( X 1 , Y 1 ) , … , ( X n , Y n ) (X_{1},Y_{1}),\ldots,(X_{n},Y_{n}) according to model ( 1 ), where the regression coefficients b ∗ ∈ ℝ d × p b^{*}\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times p} has independent 𝒩 ⁡ ( 5 , 5 ) \mathcal{N}(5,5) entries and is kept fixed for all repetitions. Covariates X i ∈ ℝ p X_{i}\in\mathbb{R}^{p} , i = 1 , … , n i=1,\ldots,n , are drawn from N ⁡ ( 0 , Σ ) N(0,\Sigma) with a Toeplitz covariance matrix Σ = ( 2 − | i − j | ) i , j ∈ ℝ p × p \Sigma=(2^{-|i-j|})_{i,j}\in\mathbb{R}^{p\times p} . The noise ε \varepsilon is generated from one of the following distributions: (1a) ε ∼ 𝒩 ⁡ ( 0 , I d ) \varepsilon\sim\mathcal{N}(0,I_{d}) (1b) ε ∼ t 2 ​ ( 0 , I d ) \varepsilon\sim t_{2}(0,I_{d}) follows a multivariate t 2 t_{2} distribution (1c) ε \varepsilon has each marginal distributed with Pareto ⁡ ( − 2 , 2 , 1 ) \mathrm{Pareto}(-2,2,1) 1 1 1 the Pareto distribution Pareto ⁡ ( k , α , s ) \mathrm{Pareto}(k,\alpha,s) has density function f ⁡ ( x ) ∝ α ​ s α + 1 ( x − k ) α + 1 f(x)\varpropto\frac{\alpha s^{\alpha+1}}{(x-k)^{\alpha+1}} for all x ≥ 1 + k x\geq 1+k , with shape parameter α &gt; 0 \alpha&gt;0 , location parameter k ∈ ℝ k\in\mathbb{R} and scale parameter s &gt; 0 s&gt;0 . Here Pareto ⁡ ( − 2 , 2 , 1 ) \mathrm{Pareto}(-2,2,1) has mean 0. and the same copula as 𝒩 ⁡ ( 0 , Σ ′ ) \mathcal{N}(0,\Sigma^{\prime}) , where Σ ′ = ( 0.9 | i − j | ) i , j ∈ ℝ d × d \Sigma^{\prime}=(0.9^{|i-j|})_{i,j}\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times d} (1d) ε \varepsilon follows a centered Banana-shaped distribution, i.e. ε i = d ( B d − 1 , ‖ B d − 1 ‖ 2 − 2 d + 2 ) + 0.3 ​ B d \varepsilon_{i}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d}}{{=}}(B_{d-1},\|B_{d-1}\|^{2}-\frac{2}{d+2})+0.3B_{d} , where B d B_{d} is uniformly distributed in the unit ball in ℝ d \mathbb{R}^{d} Figure 2 reports the average matrix Mahalanobis norm error (estimated over 100 100 Monte Carlo repetitions) of MCQR, LS, SpQR and CoorCQR over the four noise distributions mentioned above for n ∈ { 100,200 , … , 600 } n\in\{100,200,\ldots,600\} and ( d , p ) ∈ { ( 2 , 7 ) , ( 4 , 10 ) } (d,p)\in\{(2,7),(4,10)\} . We see that MCQR has done well over all settings considered here. In contrast, LS estimator performs the best under Gaussian noise but has poor performance under heavy-tailed noise or noise with non-convex support. CoorCQR and SpQR have relatively good performance in panels (a) and (b) when the noise is spherically symmetric but their performance deteriorated when the noise exhibits strong cross-sectional dependence in panels (c) and (d). While our theoretical results have mostly concerned with heavy-tailed noise, we also investigate the empirical performance of MCQR in the presence of outlier contamination. Here, we consider two cases of ϵ \epsilon -contaminated noise, for some ϵ ∈ ( 0 , 1 ) \epsilon\in(0,1) : (2a) ε ∼ ( 1 − ϵ ) ​ P 1 + ϵ ​ P 2 \varepsilon\sim(1-\epsilon)P_{1}+\epsilon P_{2} ; here P 1 P_{1} is a Pareto copula with Pareto ⁡ ( − 10 9 , 10 , 1 ) \mathrm{Pareto}(-\frac{10}{9},10,1) marginals and copula generated by 𝒩 ⁡ ( 0 , Σ ′ ) \mathcal{N}(0,\Sigma^{\prime}) as in case (1c) and P 2 P_{2} is a heavier-tailed location-shifted Pareto copula with marginals distributed as Pareto ⁡ ( 10 , 2 , 10 ) \mathrm{Pareto}(10,2,10) . (2b) ε ∼ ( 1 − ϵ ) ​ 𝒩 ​ ( 0 , I d ) + ϵ ​ 𝒩 ​ ( 100 , I d ) \varepsilon\sim(1-\epsilon)\mathcal{N}(0,I_{d})+\epsilon\mathcal{N}(100,I_{d}) (a) Pareto contamination (b) Gaussian contamination Figure 3: Logarithmic average estimation loss, measured in matrix Mahalanobis norm, of the regression coefficient estimated by MCQR, CoorCQR, SpQR and LS for data generated according to the mechanism described in Section 4 for various outlier contamination proportion (from 0.05 0.05 to 0.5 0.5 ), covariate dimension p p and response dimension d d and two different noise contamination models. We fix n = 200 n=200 . Figure 3 shows the performance of the four procedures for increasing levels of contamination proportion ϵ \epsilon . We observe that MCQR is generally more robust than other competitors when we add additional outliers to the random error. Interestingly, we see that in the case where d = 1 d=1 , the CoorCQR, which reduces to the univariate CQR, shows a lack of robustness against the outlier contamination, while the 1-dimensional version of MCQR maintains its robustness even with a high proportion of contamination. A.1 Preliminaries on optimal transport theory Define the rescaled squared ℓ 2 \ell_{2} -distance as L 2 ​ ( x , y ) := 1 2 ​ ‖ x − y ‖ 2 L_{2}(x,y):=\frac{1}{2}\|x-y\|^{2} for any x , y ∈ ℝ d x,y\in\mathbb{R}^{d} . In this notation, for two distributions P P and Q Q on ℝ d \mathbb{R}^{d} , we have 1 2 ​ 𝒲 2 2 ​ ( P , Q ) = inf γ ∈ 𝒞 ⁡ ( P , Q ) ∫ L 2 ​ ( x , y ) ​ 𝑑 γ ​ ( x , y ) = : I 2 ​ ( P , Q ) . \displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\mathcal{W}_{2}^{2}(P,Q)=\inf_{\gamma\in\mathcal{C}(P,Q)}\int L_{2}(x,y)d\gamma(x,y)=:I_{2}(P,Q). (15) Our proof depends on the following Kantorovich duality (see e.g., Villani 2021a , Theorem 1.3) I 2 ​ ( P , Q ) = sup φ , ψ ∈ Φ 2 J P , Q ​ ( φ , ψ ) , I_{2}(P,Q)=\sup_{\varphi,\psi\in\Phi_{2}}J_{P,Q}(\varphi,\psi), (16) where Φ 2 := { ( φ , ψ ) ∈ L 1 ​ ( P ) × L 1 ​ ( Q ) : φ ⁡ ( x ) + ψ ⁡ ( y ) ≤ L 2 ​ ( x , y ) } \Phi_{2}:=\{(\varphi,\psi)\in L^{1}(P)\times L^{1}(Q):\varphi(x)+\psi(y)\leq L_{2}(x,y)\} and J P , Q ​ ( φ , ψ ) := ∫ φ ⁡ ( x ) ​ 𝑑 P ​ ( x ) + ∫ ψ ⁡ ( y ) ​ 𝑑 Q ​ ( y ) . J_{P,Q}(\varphi,\psi):=\int\varphi(x)dP(x)+\int\psi(y)dQ(y). By taking advantage of the particular form of L 2 L_{2} , we also have for Φ ~ := { ( φ , ψ ) ∈ L 1 ​ ( P ) × L 1 ​ ( Q ) : φ ⁡ ( x ) + ψ ⁡ ( y ) ≥ x T ​ y } \tilde{\Phi}:=\{(\varphi,\psi)\in L^{1}(P)\times L^{1}(Q):\varphi(x)+\psi(y)\geq x^{T}y\} that ∫ ‖ x ‖ 2 2 ​ 𝑑 P ​ ( x ) + ∫ ‖ y ‖ 2 2 ​ 𝑑 Q ​ ( y ) − sup φ , ψ ∈ Φ 2 J P , Q ​ ( φ , ψ ) = inf φ , ψ ∈ Φ ~ J P , Q ​ ( φ , ψ ) := I ~ 2 ​ ( P , Q ) . \displaystyle\int\frac{\|x\|^{2}}{2}dP(x)+\int\frac{\|y\|^{2}}{2}dQ(y)-\sup_{\varphi,\psi\in\Phi_{2}}J_{P,Q}(\varphi,\psi)=\inf_{\varphi,\psi\in\tilde{\Phi}}J_{P,Q}(\varphi,\psi):=\tilde{I}_{2}(P,Q). (17) Thus solve the problem of ( 16 ) degenerates to solve the problem of I ~ 2 ​ ( P , Q ) \tilde{I}_{2}(P,Q) . For any φ ∈ L 1 ​ ( P ) \varphi\in L^{1}(P) , define its Legendre transform as φ ∗ ​ ( y ) := sup x ∈ ℝ d ( x T ​ y − φ ⁡ ( x ) ) \varphi^{*}(y):=\sup_{x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}}(x^{T}y-\varphi(x)) . Then it can be shown that φ ∗ \varphi^{*} is a convex lower semi-continuous (l.s.c.) function. This definition immediately implies that for any ( φ , ψ ) ∈ Φ ~ (\varphi,\psi)\in\tilde{\Phi} , ψ ⁡ ( y ) ≥ φ ∗ ​ ( y ) , ∀ y ∈ ℝ d . \psi(y)\geq\varphi^{*}(y),\ \forall y\in\mathbb{R}^{d}. Thus we have J P , Q ​ ( φ , ψ ) ≥ J P , Q ​ ( φ , φ ∗ ) J_{P,Q}(\varphi,\psi)\geq J_{P,Q}(\varphi,\varphi^{*}) . Similarily, we have φ ⁡ ( x ) ≥ sup y ∈ ℝ d ( x T ​ y − φ ∗ ​ ( y ) ) = φ ∗ ⁣ ∗ ​ ( x ) , ∀ x ∈ ℝ d , \varphi(x)\geq\sup_{y\in\mathbb{R}^{d}}\bigl(x^{T}y-\varphi^{*}(y)\bigr)=\varphi^{**}(x),\ \forall x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}, which further implies that J P , Q ​ ( φ , φ ∗ ) ≥ J P , Q ​ ( φ ∗ ⁣ ∗ , φ ∗ ) J_{P,Q}(\varphi,\varphi^{*})\geq J_{P,Q}(\varphi^{**},\varphi^{*}) . In the end, we deduced that inf φ , ψ ∈ Φ ~ J P , Q ​ ( φ , ψ ) ≥ inf φ ∈ L 1 ​ ( P ) J P , Q ​ ( φ ∗ ⁣ ∗ , φ ∗ ) ≥ inf φ is convex l.s.c. J P , Q ​ ( φ ∗ , φ ) . \inf_{\varphi,\psi\in\tilde{\Phi}}J_{P,Q}(\varphi,\psi)\geq\inf_{\varphi\in L^{1}(P)}J_{P,Q}(\varphi^{**},\varphi^{*})\geq\inf_{\text{$\varphi$ is convex l.s.c.}}J_{P,Q}(\varphi^{*},\varphi). In fact, it can be shown \Citep [see e.g.][Theorem 2.9]villani2021topics that the equality above holds, i.e. ​there exists a convex l.s.c. ​function φ 0 \varphi_{0} such that the conjugate pair ( φ 0 , φ 0 ∗ ) (\varphi_{0},\varphi_{0}^{*}) is the optimal solution to I ~ 2 ​ ( P , Q ) \tilde{I}_{2}(P,Q) . Now we are ready to state a fundemental theorem for the optimal transport theory with L 2 L_{2} loss function. Theorem 9 . ( Villani 2021a , Theorem 2.12 and Remark 2.13(iii)) Let P P and Q Q be probability measures on ℝ d \mathbb{R}^{d} , with finite second moment. We consider the Kantorovich dual problem associated with the rescaled squared ℓ 2 \ell_{2} -distance L 2 L_{2} . Then γ ∈ 𝒞 ⁡ ( P , Q ) \gamma\in\mathcal{C}(P,Q) is optimal if and only if there exists a convex l.s.c. ​function φ 0 \varphi_{0} such that Supp ⁡ ( γ ) ⊂ ∂ φ 0 , \mathrm{Supp}(\gamma)\subset\partial\varphi_{0}, or equivalently, for γ \gamma -almost all ( x , y ) (x,y) , y ∈ ∂ φ 0 ​ ( x ) . y\in\partial\varphi_{0}(x). Moreover, there exists a conjugate pair ( φ 0 , φ 0 ∗ ) (\varphi_{0},\varphi_{0}^{*}) that is a minimizer of I ~ 2 ​ ( P , Q ) \tilde{I}_{2}(P,Q) . Thus ( ∥ ⋅ ∥ 2 / 2 − φ 0 , ∥ ⋅ ∥ 2 / 2 − φ 0 ∗ ) (\|\cdot\|^{2}/2-\varphi_{0},\|\cdot\|^{2}/2-\varphi_{0}^{*}) solves the Kantorovich dual problem I 2 ​ ( P , Q ) I_{2}(P,Q) . The 1 1 -Wasserstein distance satisfies the following Kantorovich–Rubinstein duality. Theorem 10 (Kantorovich–Rubinstein theorem) . Suppose 𝒳 \mathcal{X} is a subset of ℝ d \mathbb{R}^{d} , define the diameter of 𝒳 \mathcal{X} as diam ⁡ ( 𝒳 ) := sup x , y ∈ 𝒳 ‖ x − y ‖ \mathrm{diam}(\mathcal{X}):=\sup_{x,y\in\mathcal{X}}\|x-y\| . Let Lip ⁡ ( 𝒳 ) \mathrm{Lip}(\mathcal{X}) denote the space of all Lipschitz function on 𝒳 \mathcal{X} and for any f f within this space define ‖ f ‖ Lip ⁡ ( 𝒳 ) := max ⁡ { sup x , y ∈ 𝒳 x ≠ y | f ⁡ ( x ) − f ⁡ ( y ) | ‖ x − y ‖ , ‖ f ‖ ∞ diam ⁡ ( 𝒳 ) } . \|f\|_{\mathrm{Lip}(\mathcal{X})}:=\max\Bigl\{\sup_{\begin{subarray}{c}x,y\in\mathcal{X}\\ x\not=y\end{subarray}}\frac{|f(x)-f(y)|}{\|x-y\|},\ \frac{\|f\|_{\infty}}{\mathrm{diam}(\mathcal{X})}\Bigr\}. Then 𝒲 1 ( P , Q ) = sup { ∫ f ( x ) d P ( x ) − ∫ f ( y ) d Q ( y ) : f ∈ L 1 ( | P − Q | ) , f ∈ Lip 1 ( 𝒳 ) } , \displaystyle\mathcal{W}_{1}(P,Q)=\sup\Biggl\{\int f(x)dP(x)-\int f(y)dQ(y):f\in\mathrm{L}^{1}(|P-Q|),\ f\in\mathrm{Lip}_{1}(\mathcal{X})\Biggr\}, (18) where Lip 1 ​ ( 𝒳 ) := { f : ‖ f ‖ Lip ⁡ ( 𝒳 ) ≤ 1 } \mathrm{Lip}_{1}(\mathcal{X}):=\{f:\|f\|_{\mathrm{Lip}(\mathcal{X})}\leq 1\} . In particular, the 1 1 -Wasserstein distance can be seen as a special case of a integral probability metric (defined below) with respect to the Lip 1 \mathrm{Lip}_{1} function class. Definition 11 (Integral Probability Metrics) . Given probability measures P P and Q Q as before, the integral probability metrics (IPMs) with respect to function class ℱ \mathcal{F} is defined as IPM ⁡ ( P , Q , ℱ ) = sup f ∈ ℱ { ∫ f ⁡ ( x ) ​ 𝑑 P ​ ( x ) − ∫ f ⁡ ( y ) ​ 𝑑 Q ​ ( y ) } . \mathrm{IPM}(P,Q;\mathcal{F})=\sup_{f\in\mathcal{F}}\Bigl\{\int f(x)dP(x)-\int f(y)dQ(y)\Bigr\}. (19) A.2 Additional notation Suppose T T is a map from a measurable space X X , equipped with a measure μ \mu , to an arbitrary space Y Y , we denote by T ​ # ​ μ T\#\mu as the push-forward of μ \mu by T T . Specifically, ( T ​ # ​ μ ) ​ ( A ) = μ ⁡ ( T − 1 ​ ( A ) ) (T\#\mu)(A)=\mu(T^{-1}(A)) for any measurable set A A . Suppose X 1 , … , X n X_{1},\ldots,X_{n} are random samples from some probability distribution P P . Then given any function class ℱ \mathcal{F} , define the Rademacher complexity of ℱ \mathcal{F} as ℛ n ​ ( ℱ , P ) := 𝔼 ⁡ ( sup f ∈ ℱ 1 n ​ ∑ i = 1 n ξ i ​ f ​ ( X i ) ) , \mathcal{R}_{n}(\mathcal{F},P):=\E\Bigl(\sup_{f\in\mathcal{F}}\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\xi_{i}f(X_{i})\Bigr), (20) where ξ i \xi_{i} ’s are independent Rademacher random variables, independent from X 1 , … , X n X_{1},\ldots,X_{n} . The p p -dimensional closed ball in centered at x ∈ ℝ p x\in\mathbb{R}^{p} with radius r &gt; 0 r&gt;0 is denoted by ℬ x , r p := { y ∈ ℝ p : ‖ y ‖ ≤ r } \mathcal{B}_{x,r}^{p}:=\{y\in\mathbb{R}^{p}:\|y\|\leq r\} and we omit r r when r = 1 r=1 : ℬ x , 1 p := ℬ x p \mathcal{B}_{x,1}^{p}:=\mathcal{B}_{x}^{p} . The matrix operator norm is denoted by ∥ ⋅ ∥ op \|\cdot\|_{\mathrm{op}} , so that ∥ A ∥ op := sup x : ‖ x ‖ = 1 ∥ A x ∥ \|A\|_{\mathrm{op}}:=\sup_{x:\|x\|=1}\|Ax\| . A.3 Proof for Lemma 1 Proof. For any fixed τ ∈ ( 0 , 1 ) \tau\in(0,1) , by the definition of check function ρ τ \rho_{\tau} we have q Y ​ ( τ ) ∈ arg ​ min θ ​ 𝔼 ​ ρ τ ​ ( Y − θ ) , q_{Y}(\tau)\in\argmin_{\theta}\E\rho_{\tau}(Y-\theta), where q Y ​ ( ⋅ ) q_{Y}(\cdot) is the quantile function of Y Y . Thus under the linear model ( 1 ) we have for any x ∈ ℝ p x\in\mathbb{R}^{p} , ( b ∗ , q ε ∗ ​ ( τ ) ) ∈ arg ​ min b ∈ ℝ 1 × d , q ∈ ℝ ​ 𝔼 ​ [ ρ τ ​ ( Y − bX − q ) ∣ X = x ] . \displaystyle(b^{*},q_{\varepsilon}^{*}(\tau))\in\argmin_{b\in\mathbb{R}^{1\times d},q\in\mathbb{R}}\E[\rho_{\tau}(Y-bX-q)\mid X=x]. (21) For any b ∈ ℝ 1 × p b\in\mathbb{R}^{1\times p} and q ∈ ℝ q\in\mathbb{R} , define g ⁡ ( x , b , q ) := 𝔼 ⁡ [ ρ τ ​ ( Y − bX − q ) ∣ X = x ] g(x;b,q):=\E[\rho_{\tau}(Y-bX-q)\mid X=x] , then ( 21 ) implies that g ⁡ ( x , b ∗ , q ε ∗ ​ ( τ ) ) ≤ g ⁡ ( x , b , q ) , g(x;b^{*},q_{\varepsilon}^{*}(\tau))\leq g(x;b,q), thus ∫ ℝ p g ⁡ ( x , b ∗ , q ε ∗ ​ ( τ ) ) ​ 𝑑 x ≤ ∫ ℝ p g ⁡ ( x , b , q ) ​ 𝑑 x . \int_{\mathbb{R}^{p}}g(x;b^{*},q_{\varepsilon}^{*}(\tau))\,dx\leq\int_{\mathbb{R}^{p}}g(x;b,q)\,dx. Then by the Fubini Theorem and the Law of iterated expectation, we have 𝔼 ⁡ [ ρ τ ​ ( Y − b ∗ ​ X − q ε ∗ ​ ( τ ) ) ] ≤ 𝔼 ⁡ [ ρ τ ​ ( Y − bX − q ) ] . \displaystyle\E[\rho_{\tau}(Y-b^{*}X-q_{\varepsilon}^{*}(\tau))]\leq\E[\rho_{\tau}(Y-bX-q)]. Let ( 𝒳 i , Ω i , ν i ) (\mathcal{X}_{i},\Omega_{i},\nu_{i}) , i ∈ V i\in V be L L probability spaces. Suppose that for some E ⊆ V × V E\subseteq V\times V , and for each ( i , j ) ∈ E (i,j)\in E , we have a pre-specified joint probability measure ξ i , j \xi_{i,j} on ( 𝒳 i × 𝒳 j , Ω i ⊗ Ω j ) (\mathcal{X}_{i}\times\mathcal{X}_{j},\Omega_{i}\otimes\Omega_{j}) such that ξ i , j | 𝒳 i = ν i \xi_{i,j}|_{\mathcal{X}_{i}}=\nu_{i} and ξ i , j | 𝒳 j = ν j \xi_{i,j}|_{\mathcal{X}_{j}}=\nu_{j} . If the simple undirected graph G = ( V , E ) G=(V,E) is acyclic, then there exists a joint probability measure ρ \rho on ( ∏ i = 1 L 𝒳 i , ⨂ i = 1 L Ω i ) \bigl(\prod_{i=1}^{L}\mathcal{X}_{i},\bigotimes_{i=1}^{L}\Omega_{i}\bigr) such that ρ | 𝒳 i = ν i \rho|_{\mathcal{X}_{i}}=\nu_{i} for all i ∈ V i\in V and ρ | 𝒳 i × 𝒳 j = ξ i , j \rho|_{\mathcal{X}_{i}\times\mathcal{X}_{j}}=\xi_{i,j} for all ( i , j ) ∈ E (i,j)\in E . Proof. We assume first that G G is connected. Then, there exists a traversal of all the vertices in G G such that apart from the first vertex in the traversal, each vertex has exactly one edge connected to an earlier vertex. This can be done by using e.g. depth-first search or breadth first search, after arbitrarily assigning a root node, and each node is connected only to its parent node when first visited. Hence, without loss of generality, we may relabel the nodes so that this traversal is given by the ordering 1 , 2 , … , L 1,2,\ldots,L . We now prove by induction that for any ℓ ∈ { 1 , … , L } \ell\in\{1,\ldots,L\} , there exists a measure ρ 1 , … , ℓ \rho_{1,\ldots,\ell} on 𝒳 1 × ⋯ × 𝒳 ℓ \mathcal{X}_{1}\times\cdots\times\mathcal{X}_{\ell} such that ρ 1 , … , ℓ | 𝒳 i = ν i \rho_{1,\ldots,\ell}|_{\mathcal{X}_{i}}=\nu_{i} for all i ∈ { 1 , … , ℓ } i\in\{1,\ldots,\ell\} and ρ 1 , … , ℓ | 𝒳 i × 𝒳 j = ξ i , j \rho_{1,\ldots,\ell}|_{\mathcal{X}_{i}\times\mathcal{X}_{j}}=\xi_{i,j} for all ( i , j ) ∈ E ∩ { 1 , … , ℓ } 2 (i,j)\in E\cap\{1,\ldots,\ell\}^{2} . The base case of the induction is trivially true as we can take ρ 1 = ν 1 \rho_{1}=\nu_{1} . Now assume that we have successfully constructed ρ 1 , … , ℓ − 1 \rho_{1,\ldots,\ell-1} for some ℓ ∈ { 2 , … , L } \ell\in\{2,\ldots,L\} . Let ℓ ′ \ell^{\prime} be the only neighbour of ℓ \ell in { 1 , … , ℓ − 1 } \{1,\ldots,\ell-1\} (the existence and uniqueness of ℓ ′ \ell^{\prime} is guaranteed by the traversal ordering of the vertices in the previous paragraph). By the Disintegration Theorem (see e.g. Graf &amp; Mauldin 1989a ) , there exists a probability measure ξ ℓ | ℓ ′ ( ⋅ ∣ x ℓ ′ ) \xi_{\ell\mid\ell^{\prime}}(\cdot\mid x_{\ell^{\prime}}) on 𝒳 ℓ \mathcal{X}_{\ell} such that d ​ ξ ℓ | ℓ ′ ​ ( x ℓ ∣ x ℓ ′ ) ​ d ​ ν ℓ ′ ​ ( x ℓ ′ ) = d ​ ξ ℓ ′ , ℓ ​ ( x ℓ ′ , x ℓ ) d\xi_{\ell\mid\ell^{\prime}}(x_{\ell}\mid x_{\ell^{\prime}})d\nu_{\ell^{\prime}}(x_{\ell^{\prime}})=d\xi_{\ell^{\prime},\ell}(x_{\ell^{\prime}},x_{\ell}) . Now, we define d ​ ρ 1 , … , ℓ ​ ( x 1 , … , x ℓ ) = d ​ ρ 1 , … , ℓ − 1 ​ ( x 1 , … , x ℓ − 1 ) ​ d ​ ξ ℓ | ℓ ′ ​ ( x ℓ ∣ x ℓ ′ ) . d\rho_{1,\ldots,\ell}(x_{1},\ldots,x_{\ell})=d\rho_{1,\ldots,\ell-1}(x_{1},\ldots,x_{\ell-1})d\xi_{\ell\mid\ell^{\prime}}(x_{\ell}\mid x_{\ell^{\prime}}). Then the conclusion follows by Villani 2021a , Proposition 2.4 . ∎ Now we argue that for all b ∈ ℬ b\in\mathcal{B} , φ b ; n , m ∗ \varphi_{b;n,m}^{*} (and similarly, φ b ; n , m \varphi_{b;n,m} ) is a piecewise Lipschitz function on a high probability event that does not depend on b b . The following lemma plays a key role in the argument. It implies that the local Lipschitz constant of φ b ; n , m ∗ \varphi_{b;n,m}^{*} is largely driven by the magnitude of the subdifferential of φ b ; n , m ∗ \varphi_{b;n,m}^{*} . The proof is analogous to \Citet [Lemma 10]manole2021sharp, but for the sake of completeness, we provide it here. Lemma 14 . Suppose P P and Q Q are two distributions on ℝ d \mathbb{R}^{d} . Then for any r ≥ 1 r\geq 1 , φ 0 : ℬ 0 , r d → ℝ \varphi_{0}:\mathcal{B}_{0,r}^{d}\to\mathbb{R} and φ 0 ∗ : ℬ 0 , r d → ℝ \varphi_{0}^{*}:\mathcal{B}_{0,r}^{d}\to\mathbb{R} are Lipschitz continuous with parameters L 0 L_{0} and L 0 ∗ L_{0}^{*} respectively, where L 0 := sup { ‖ y ‖ : y ∈ ∂ φ 0 ​ ( ℬ 0 , r d ) } , and L 0 ∗ := sup { ‖ z ‖ : z ∈ ∂ φ 0 ∗ ​ ( ℬ 0 , r d ) } \displaystyle L_{0}:=\sup\bigl\{\|y\|:y\in\partial\varphi_{0}\bigl(\mathcal{B}_{0,r}^{d}\bigr)\bigr\}\quad,\text{and }\quad L_{0}^{*}:=\sup\bigl\{\|z\|:z\in\partial\varphi_{0}^{*}(\mathcal{B}_{0,r}^{d})\bigr\} Proof. Proposition 17 . There exists a constant C &gt; 0 C&gt;0 depending on d , α , β , σ 1 , σ 2 , γ 1 , γ 2 d,\alpha,\beta,\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2},\gamma_{1},\gamma_{2} such that for any fixed n , m ≥ 1 n,m\geq 1 , the following inequality holds sup b ∈ ℬ | 𝒲 2 2 ​ ( P A ⁡ ( b ) , P U ) − 𝒲 2 2 ​ ( P n A ⁡ ( b ) , P m U ) | ≤ C ​ ( log ⁡ m ) 8 2 ∧ α ∧ β ​ ( p n + 1 n 2 / d ) \displaystyle\sup_{b\in\mathcal{B}}\bigl|\mathcal{W}_{2}^{2}(P^{A(b)},P^{U})-\mathcal{W}_{2}^{2}(P_{n}^{A(b)},P_{m}^{U})\bigr|\leq C(\log m)^{\frac{8}{2\wedge\alpha\wedge\beta}}\biggl(\sqrt{\frac{p}{n}}+\frac{1}{n^{2/d}}\biggr) (55) with probability at least 1 − 29 ​ ( log ⁡ n ) − 1 1-29(\log n)^{-1} . Proof. (77) Moreover, note φ ~ ​ ( x ) = ‖ x ‖ 2 / 2 − φ 0 ​ ( x ) = ‖ x ‖ 2 / 2 − inf y ∈ 𝒴 { c ⁡ ( x , y ) − φ 0 c ​ ( y ) } = sup y ∈ 𝒴 { x T ​ y − ( ‖ y ‖ 2 / 2 − φ 0 c ​ ( y ) ) } , \displaystyle\tilde{\varphi}(x)=\|x\|^{2}/2-\varphi_{0}(x)=\|x\|^{2}/2-\inf_{y\in\mathcal{Y}}\{c(x,y)-\varphi_{0}^{c}(y)\}=\sup_{y\in\mathcal{Y}}\{x^{T}y-(\|y\|^{2}/2-\varphi_{0}^{c}(y))\}, which implies that ( φ ~ , ψ ~ ) ∈ Φ ~ (\tilde{\varphi},\tilde{\psi})\in\tilde{\Phi} . Combine this with ( 77 ), we proved that ( φ ~ , ψ ~ ) (\tilde{\varphi},\tilde{\psi}) is an optimal solution to the left-hand side of ( 75 ). Finally, by the boundedness of φ 0 \varphi_{0} and φ 0 c \varphi_{0}^{c} , we have 0 ≤ ‖ x ‖ 2 / 2 − φ ~ ​ ( x ) ≤ ‖ L 2 ‖ ∞ ​ and − ‖ L 2 ‖ ∞ ≤ ‖ y ‖ 2 / 2 − ψ ~ ​ ( y ) ≤ 0 , \displaystyle 0\leq\|x\|^{2}/2-\tilde{\varphi}(x)\leq\|L_{2}\|_{\infty}\ \text{and}\ -\|L_{2}\|_{\infty}\leq\|y\|^{2}/2-\tilde{\psi}(y)\leq 0, as desired. ∎ Theorem 29 . ( Fournier &amp; Guillin 2015c , Theorem 1) Let X ∼ P X X\sim P^{X} be a probability measure on ℝ d \mathbb{R}^{d} such that M ℓ := 𝔼 ⁡ ‖ X ‖ ℓ &lt; + ∞ M_{\ell}:=\E\|X\|^{\ell}&lt;+\infty with ℓ ∈ ( 2 , + ∞ ) \ell\in(2,+\infty) . If P n X P_{n}^{X} is the corresponding empirical distribution, then there exists a constant C &gt; 0 C&gt;0 depending only on d d and ℓ \ell such that for all n ≥ 1 n\geq 1 , 𝔼 ⁡ [ 𝒲 2 2 ​ ( P X , P n X ) ] ≤ CM ℓ 2 / ℓ ​ τ n ​ ( d , ℓ ) , \displaystyle\E\left[\mathcal{W}_{2}^{2}(P^{X},P_{n}^{X})\right]\leq CM_{\ell}^{2/\ell}\tau_{n}(d,\ell), (78) where τ n ​ ( d , ℓ ) := { n − 1 2 if d &lt; 4 n − 1 2 ​ log ⁡ ( 1 + n ) if d = 4 n − 2 d if d &gt; 4 + { n − 1 d if ℓ &gt; 4 n − 1 2 ​ log ⁡ ( 1 + n ) if ℓ = 4 n 2 − ℓ ℓ if 2 &lt; ℓ &lt; 4 . \displaystyle\tau_{n}(d,\ell):=\begin{cases}n^{-\frac{1}{2}}&amp;\text{ if $d&lt;4$}\\ n^{-\frac{1}{2}}\log(1+n)&amp;\text{if $d=4$}\\ n^{-\frac{2}{d}}&amp;\text{if $d&gt;4$}\end{cases}+\begin{cases}n^{-\frac{1}{d}}&amp;\text{if $\ell&gt;4$}\\ n^{-\frac{1}{2}}\log(1+n)&amp;\text{if $\ell=4$}\\ n^{\frac{2-\ell}{\ell}}&amp;\text{if $2&lt;\ell&lt;4$}.\end{cases} Appendix C Spatial reference distribution In this section, we derive the MCQR loss function under reference distribution U ⁡ [ − 1 , 1 ] U[-1,1] , which may provide an intuitive example for the verification of Proposition 3 . In one dimension, the traditional rank and quantile can be understood as a pair of optimal transport maps between the distribution of interest X ∼ P X\sim P and the uniform distribution U ∼ U ⁡ [ 0 , 1 ] U\sim U[0,1] . When P P does not assign mass to sets with Hausdorff dimension 0 0 , the corresponding distribution function F F and its inverse map Q := F − 1 Q:=F^{-1} serve as the corresponding optimal transport map. This concept can be generalized to other reference distributions, for instance, U ⁡ [ − 1 , 1 ] U[-1,1] . In this case, the spatial distribution function F sp ​ ( ⋅ ) := 2 ​ F ​ ( ⋅ ) − 1 F_{\mathrm{sp}}(\cdot):=2F(\cdot)-1 takes on the role of F F in the previous case. Moreover, the corresponding check function needs to be modified as ρ τ sp ( X − θ ) := ( 1 + τ ) ( X − θ ) − 2 ( X − θ ) 𝟙 { X − θ &lt; 0 } , ∀ τ ∈ [ − 1 , 1 ] . \displaystyle\rho_{\tau}^{\mathrm{sp}}(X-\theta):=(1+\tau)(X-\theta)-2(X-\theta)\one\{X-\theta&lt;0\},\quad\forall\tau\in[-1,1]. Suppose V ∼ U ⁡ [ − 1 , 1 ] V\sim U[-1,1] , then the composite quantile regression optimization becomes 𝔼 ∫ − 1 1 ρ τ sp ( Y − β ⊤ X \displaystyle\E\int_{-1}^{1}\rho_{\tau}^{\mathrm{sp}}\bigl(Y-\beta^{\top}X − q ( τ ) ) ⋅ 1 2 d τ = 𝔼 ∫ − 1 1 ( Y − β ⊤ X − q ( τ ) ) − d τ + ∫ − 1 1 ∫ τ − 1 1 2 q ( τ ) dtd τ \displaystyle-q(\tau)\bigr)\cdot\frac{1}{2}\,d\tau=\E\int_{-1}^{1}\bigl(Y-\beta^{\top}X-q(\tau)\bigr)^{-}\,d\tau+\int_{-1}^{1}\int_{\tau}^{-1}\frac{1}{2}q(\tau)\,dtd\tau = 𝔼 max t ∈ [ − 1 , 1 ] ∫ t 1 − ( Y − bX − q ( τ ) ) d τ + ∫ − 1 1 ∫ t 1 1 2 q ( τ ) d τ dt \displaystyle=\E\max_{t\in[-1,1]}\int_{t}^{1}-\bigl(Y-bX-q(\tau)\bigr)\,d\tau+\int_{-1}^{1}\int_{t}^{1}\frac{1}{2}q(\tau)\,d\tau dt = 𝔼 ⁡ max t ∈ [ − 1 , 1 ] ⁡ ( − ( 1 − t ) ​ ( Y − bX ) + ϕ ⁡ ( t ) ) + 𝔼 ⁡ ϕ ⁡ ( V ) \displaystyle=\E\max_{t\in[-1,1]}\bigl(-(1-t)(Y-bX)+\phi(t)\bigr)+\E\phi(V) = 𝔼 ⁡ max t ∈ [ − 1 , 1 ] ⁡ ( t ⁡ ( Y − bX ) + ϕ ⁡ ( t ) ) + 𝔼 ⁡ ϕ ⁡ ( V ) , \displaystyle=\E\max_{t\in[-1,1]}\bigl(t(Y-bX)+\phi(t)\bigr)+\E\phi(V), where ϕ ⁡ ( t ) = ∫ t 1 q ⁡ ( τ ) ​ 𝑑 τ . \phi(t)=\int_{t}^{1}q(\tau)d\tau. Thus, applying the same argument as Lemma 2 we can see that the composition quantile regression estimator of b ∗ b^{*} is once again b ∗ = arg ​ min ⁡ ⟨ ⟨ P Y − bX , P V ⟩ ⟩ 𝒲 2 . \displaystyle b^{*}=\argmin\langle\!\langle P^{Y-bX},P^{V}\rangle\!\rangle_{\mathcal{W}_{2}}. This gives some intuition on Proposition 3 . However, if choose the standard normal distribution as the reference distribution, we may not be able to find a straightforward optimal transport map as F F or F sp F_{\mathrm{sp}} , but Proposition 3 demonstrates the validity of this extension. Appendix D Spatial quantile The concept of the spatial (or geometric) quantile was initially introduced by Chaudhuri 1996c . Uniquely characterizing the underlying probability distribution as a special case of M-quantile, as demonstrated in ( Koltchinskii 1997c , Theorem 2.5) , this quantile permits a seamless extension to the regression framework ( Chakraborty 2003a ) and functional quantile regression ( Chakraborty &amp; Chaudhuri 2014c ; Chowdhury &amp; Chaudhuri 2019a ) . A more recent development involves an extension to the hypersphere, as explored by Konen &amp; Paindaveine 2023a . The definition of Spatial quantile starts from rewriting the check function ρ τ ​ ( ⋅ ) \rho_{\tau}(\cdot) as ρ τ ​ ( z ) \displaystyle\rho_{\tau}(z) = 1 2 ​ ( | z | + ( 2 ​ τ − 1 ) ​ z ) = 1 2 ​ ( | z | + v ​ z ) , for any z ∈ ℝ , \displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\bigl(|z|+(2\tau-1)z\bigr)=\frac{1}{2}(|z|+vz),\ \text{for any $z\in\mathbb{R}$}, with v = 2 ​ τ − 1 v=2\tau-1 . Thus a natural extension of the check function to the multi-dimensional case is by substituting the absolute value function by the L 1 L_{1} -loss function: Φ v ​ ( z ) := 1 2 ​ ( ‖ z ‖ + v ⊤ ​ z ) , \displaystyle\Phi_{v}(z):=\frac{1}{2}(\|z\|+v^{\top}z), where v = τ ​ u v=\tau u , and u ∈ 𝒮 d − 1 u\in\mathcal{S}^{d-1} . This extension of the check function immediately leads to the following definition of spatial quantile: Definition 30 . Suppose Y ∼ ℙ Y Y\sim\Prob^{Y} is a random variable on ℝ d \mathbb{R}^{d} ( d ≥ 1 ) (d\geq 1) . Then for any τ ∈ [ 0 , 1 ] \tau\in[0,1] and u ∈ 𝒮 d − 1 u\in\mathcal{S}^{d-1} , the τ ​ u \tau u -spatial quantile of P Y P^{Y} is defined as Q τ ​ u = arg ​ min y ∈ ℝ d ​ 𝔼 ​ Φ τ ​ u ​ ( Y − y ) . \displaystyle\mathrm{Q}_{\tau u}=\argmin_{y\in\mathbb{R}^{d}}\E\Phi_{\tau u}(Y-y). (79) Note the solution of ( 79 ) are such that 𝔼 ⁡ ( Y − Q τ ​ u ‖ Y − Q τ ​ u ‖ ) = − τ ​ u . \displaystyle\E\bigl(\frac{Y-Q_{\tau u}}{\|Y-Q_{\tau u}\|}\bigr)=-\tau u. Intuitively speaking, this indicates that Q τ ​ u Q_{\tau u} defines a point in ℝ d \mathbb{R}^{d} such that the average unit vector from it to other random samples should be τ ​ u \tau u . The generalization to quantile regression setting is simply by applying the spatial quantile definition to Y − b ∗ ​ X − a Y-b^{*}X-a , where X ∈ ℝ p X\in\mathbb{R}^{p} is the covariate vector, b ∗ ∈ ℝ d × p b^{*}\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times p} is the regression coefficient and a a is the intercept term. Specifically, for fixed τ ∈ [ 0 , 1 ] \tau\in[0,1] and u ∈ 𝒮 d − 1 u\in\mathcal{S}^{d-1} , ( a τ ​ u , b τ , u ) = arg ​ min b ∈ ℝ d × p , a ∈ ℝ d ​ 𝔼 ​ Φ τ ​ u ​ ( Y − bX − a ) . \displaystyle(a_{\tau u},b_{\tau,u})=\argmin_{b\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times p},\ a\in\mathbb{R}^{d}}\E\Phi_{\tau u}(Y-bX-a). Therefore, given observations ( Y 1 , X 1 ) , … , ( Y n , X n ) (Y_{1},X_{1}),\ldots,(Y_{n},X_{n}) satisfying equations Y i = b ∗ X i + ε i , i = 1 , … , n , Y_{i}=b^{*}X_{i}+\varepsilon_{i},\ i=1,\ldots,n, for some random residue terms ε i \varepsilon_{i} ’s that are independent with with X i X_{i} ’s, the spatial quantile estimator of b ∗ b^{*} can be obtained by ( b ^ ( sp ) , a ^ τ ​ u ( sp ) ) ​ arg ​ min b ∈ ℝ d × p , a ∈ ℝ p ⁡ 1 n ​ ∑ i = 1 n Φ τ ​ u ​ ( Y i − bX i − a ) . \displaystyle(\hat{b}^{(\mathrm{sp})},\hat{a}_{\tau u}^{(\mathrm{sp})})\argmin\limits_{b\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times p},\ a\in\mathbb{R}^{p}}\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\Phi_{\tau u}(Y_{i}-bX_{i}-a). Therefore, the optimizer can be obtained by applying classical convex optimization algorithms.
